INTRODUCTION. 

This celebrated collection of games was first published in 1844. 
It comprises the finest specimens of Chess play on record, from the 
time of Philidor, "the father and founder of modem Chess," to 
the period immediately preceding the publication of Staunton's 
HaTidbook. During this interval, of about half a century, the best- 
known names in the English Chess world were those of the writers 
and players, Sarratt, Lewis, Cochrane, Mouret the conductor of the 
Automaton, and the two masters MacDonnell and Labourdonnais. 
Between 1830 and 1844 we have Slous, Popert, Perigal, Walker, 
Pulling, and others named in this work, contemporaries of Staunton, 
who in 1844 had just established his reputation as the first player 
of his time, by defeating the French champion St. Amant. 

The publication of Staunton's great work, the Chess Player^s 
Handbook, in 1847, ^^ *1^® commencement of a new era in Chess. 
No previous writer had communicated so much useful information 
in a form so compact and intelligible. No other book took such 
high ground, and carried with it so much authority. It became 
immensely popular, and its influence affected the entire character 
of English practice. 

The 1020 games given in Chess Studies represent all that 
was known in Chess, by the highest mounted minds, at the time 
when Staunton began his work. They are, therefore, not only 
valuable from the historical point of view, but are in themselves 
splendid illustrations of the force of genius called into exercise by 
a struggle for supremacy in novel and difficult situations. In 
the play of MacDonnell and Labourdonnais we see the gradual 
evolution of principles, now generally accepted as almost self- 
evident, and find the rudiments of ideas which have since been 
claimed as the discoveries of later masters, possessed of less 
originality, but more industry. With regard to the stre •^h 



vi INTRODUCTION. 

in play of the two Chess kings, much has been said on both 
sides. The impression conveyed by their games is that they 
were players of the same rank and stamp, enterprising in attack, 
subtle in defence, and fully prepared for either contingency. 
Labourdonnais won the majority of games in six matches between 
them, and for that reason might justly be considered the superior 
player. He undoubtedly displayed superior generalship. There 
were, however, extenuating circumstances. MacDonnell had little 
experience of match play on even terms. His range of openings 
was limited. He played strong but risky attacks — ^the Bishop's 
Gambit, the King's Gambit in a variation which might entail 
the sacrifice of a minor piece, also his own particular Double 
Gambit. (Game No. 24.) All these broke down against 
Labourdonnais, with three notable exceptions, games Nos. 35, 
44, and 54. 

As second player, MacDonnell invariably played an open game. 
Against i P — K4 he opposed i . . , P — K4, and gave his antagonist 
the chance of continuing with the Giuoco Piano, or the Evans 
Gambit. Labourdonnais had, however, other strings to his bow. 
He knew well the method of development, described by Walker 
in Chapter X., Section VI., as the '' King's Pawn One opening 
in its several varieties " — otherwise known as the Close Game. He 
could always force it upon his opponent. He used it freely, 
both as first and second player. MacDonnell lost several games 
in trying various ways .of playing it. The fine game. No. 81, 
shows what he made of it in the end. Another of Labourdonnais' 
openings was the Queen's Gambit — a perfectly safe and sound 
attack, which, contrary to modem practice, MacDonnell always 
accepted. When, however, the latter opened with iP — Q4, 
(Game 38), Labourdonnais replied by i.., P — K3, declining the 
Gambit, and transposing into his Close Game as an alternative. 
He only once tried the King's Gambit attack against MacDonnell 
(Game No. 55). Several instances may be found in Chapter 
II., where he adopted it against less powerful antagonists. 

In the Evans Gambit both players met on level ground. It was 
first tried by MacDonnell in Game No. 26. In the fourth, fifth, 
and sixth matches it was adopted by both masters. The result of

INTRODUCTION. vii 

eleven games, and drew two, out of sixteen played, while MacDonnell 
won four out of six played, with one game drawn. As an odds- 
giver, MacDonnell ranked fully equal, if not superior, to Labour- 
donnais. Mongredien, who played at the odds of a Knight with 
both of them, says : " I fancied I had the best chance with the 
Frenchman ; " and Walker puts it far more emphatically. 

In his New Treatise on Chess, published *in 1841, Walker 
describes MacDonnell as " the first player of his day in England ; " 
and writes of Labourdonnais that "in life he was unrivalled as a 
chess-player — in death he leaves no one worthy to fill his place." 
MacDonnell died Sept. 14, 1835, aged 37; Labourdonnais, Dec. 13, 
1840, aged 43. The vacant place in English chess, notwith- 
standing Walker's lamentation, was soon occupied by Staunton. 

Whether Staunton was so strong a player as his two predeces- 
sors is questionable. He was certainly a steadier and surer player 
than MacDonnell, if less elegant in his style than Labourdonnais. 
Morphy is reported to have said of Staunton that his knowledge 
of the theory of the game was complete, and his power as an 
analyst of the very highest order, but he was deficient in 
the imaginative and creative power which conceives (or foresees) 
positions, and brings them about. "Therefore, in his best day, 
Mr. Staunton could not have made a successful fight against a 
man who had the same qualities as himself, and who, besides, was 
possessed of the creative power above mentioned. Such were 
Anderssen of Germany, MacDonnell of England (he might have 
said Ireland), and Labourdonnais of France." This is simply 
saying that Staunton, without disparagement to his genius, was a 
straightforward, practical player, who would not go out of 
his depth. The three experts last named are the most 
interesting characters in this work. It is chiefly their games that 
make it attractive to the modem school, set off, as they are, by the 
numerous lesser and side-lights afforded by correspondence games, 
games at odds, and the recorded deeds of the earlier cham- 
pions — Philidor, Verdoni, Mouret, Sarratt, Lewis, and their 
contemporaries. 

The history of Chapter VI. is thus given by Professor Tomlin- 
son : — " The automaton was worked by a French player named 
Mouret. He gave pawn and move to every comer, and out of 
300 games in the course of a few months he lost but six, and 



viii INTRODUCTION. 

these were to the well-known players of the day — Cochrane, Brand, 
and Mercier. The proprietor, M. Maelzel, allowed a number of 
the games to be taken down from actual play, and fifty of these 
were collected, in a small volume, the editor expressing a hope 
that the admirers of Philidor would not be shocked if these games 
were placed on a par with those of that great master." The 
monotony of the o'pening seems to have affected Mouret's style. 
His play is generally sound, but rarely brilliant. Nos. 316, 332, 
336, and 358 will repay examination. 

In connexion with Staunton we find the names of his most 
formidable opponents. Among them St. Amant, the French cham- 
pion, takes the lead. He was a pupil of Labourdonnais, and his 
match with Staunton was presumably too recent, when this work 
was first published, to be thought worthy of special treatment as 
a contest not only national but classical. Staunton subsequently 
did himself due honour in the Chess Players Companion, after 
his successes against Horwitz and Harrwitz had consolidated his 
position as chess champion, St. Amant was a fine player, saga- 
cious, ingenious, and careful, but slow in his movements, and in 
this respect very different from Labourdonnais. Both he and 
Staunton, in their great match, confined themselves to the Close 
Game favoured by Labourdonnais. It was in the sixth game of 
the match that Staunton improved upon Labourdonnais by playing 
his QKtP to Kt3, and following it up by B — QKt2. This deve- 
lopment, which may be traced to Verdoni, has since become an 
important feature iii all the King's Pawn One openings. Game 
No. 904 is remarkable for having occupied fourteen hours. 
Nos. 905, 934, and 913 are worthy of special attention, the last 
being " won in glorious style " by St. Amant. Chapter X. 
furnishes many examples of St. Amant's play with other strong 
players, including Walker himself. In Chapter IX. there are a 
few games at odds with Messrs. Bryan, Devinck, and Mongredien. 

Another familiar name among chess players was that of John 
Cochrane. Walker gives many specimens of his play. It has 
been said of him that he invented many ' attacks, in various 
openings, but never a sound one among them. He published a 
treatise on the game of Chess in 1822. It is not that from 
which Walker quotes twenty games (Nos. 433-452). The 



INTRODUCTION. ix 

London Ohess Club. Cochrane had a brilliant style, but he 
did not carry so heavy a lance as MacDonnell. His games are 
amusing, and when his combinations succeed against careful 
players, such as Deschapelles, Labourdonnais, Staunton, Popert, 
and St. Amant, they are admirable. Cochrane was one of the 
London players in the renowned match of five games played 
between the London and Edinburgh clubs, recorded in Chapter 
VII. He left England for India while the second game (No. 363) 
was in progress. His colleagues — Messrs. Brand, Eraser, Lewis, 
Mercier, and Pratt — after his departure contrived to throw away 
the game in a winning position. The correct moves are indicated 
in the record. The chief player on the other side was Mr. 
James Donaldson (the Bailie, not "Dielta"). He was then the 
strongest chess player in Scotland, and to him is ascribed the 
merit of winning the match. The highest praise has been 
bestowed upon Games Nos. 363 and 366. 

In 1840 Staunton, when quite a young player, won a match 
with Popert, a strong amateur, who in earlier years had played 
much with MacDonnell. Popert's practice with so aggressive an 
opponent had given him a taste for defensive operations. In 
these, according to Staunton, the ingenuity and tenacity of his 
manoeuvrings was unrivalled. Game No. 657 was prolonged to 
nearly one hundred moves. His style of play led to a remark by 
Mongredien, that when the position was critical, and required deep 
calculation, his opponent had ample time to go away, eat his 
lunch, and return before Popert had made up his mind what to 
do. On the other hand, he had the reputation of always making 
the best move in such circumstances. Walker credits him with a 
special talent for counter-attack. His record, however, does not 
represent him as a successful player. His name appears 
frequently in Chapter X. ; also in Staunton's Handbook, and 
Companion. 

Chapter X. contains also a number of games played by Mr. 
Slous, or Selous, the latter being the family name. ^He died in 
1892, at the age of 91. In the interval between the death of 
MacDonnell and the rise of Staunton, he held, with his friend 
George Walker, the primacy of English Chess. He had also pro- 
nounced literary tastes and tendencies ; wrote vers de sociAt^, and 
occasionally humorous prose. In his youth he composed a chess 



X INTRODUCTION. 

poem, in heroic measure, abounding with adjectives, entitled the 
Game of Chess, It was published in 1823, and reprinted in the 
British Chess Magazine for 1884. He abandoned active play in 
consequence of a temporary breakdown of his health. Walker 
notes (1844) that had Slous continued to practise Chess, it is 
highly probable he would have held the ground occupied by 
Mr. Staunton. 

Of Walker himself, as a chess player, it may be said that he 
was generally bright, without being especially brilliant. He 
displayed his science by avoiding hard knocks, rather than by 
administering them. His recorded games with strong players 
show that he was an adept in developing his men and making 
exchanges. When, however, his thorough knowledge of the 
principles, and how to apply them, had placed his adversary in 
his power, he proceeded to treat him to a pretty exhibition of 
skill in the Endings. Some of his games might be deleted with- 
out much disadvantage to his reputation, not that they are badly 
played, but simply because the ideas they embody have been 
illustrated in other games in a more remarkable manner. Like 
many other chess players, he thought better of the games he 
won than of those he lost. 

George Perigal was, for many years, the Honorary Secretary of 
the London Chess Club, and his name is frequently met with, in 
Chess literature, between 1834 and 1854. Mongredien, who was 
President of the club, says of him, that his games exhibit rather the 
keen polished blade of an accomplished fencer, than the heavy blows 
of a battle-axe dealt by a strong arm. Other well-known English 
players mentioned in this volume are Messrs. Sarratt and Lewis, 
the Chess writers ; Capt. Evans, R.N., the inventor of the Evans 
Gambit ; Wyvill and Williams, the second and third prize winners 
in the 185 1 Tournament ; Daniels, whose play — in the style of Coch- 
rane — was much admired; .Brand, Mercier, Matthews, Newham, 
Buckle, author of the History of Civilisation, Pulling, Eraser (not 
Geo. B.), Finch, Tavemer, Worrall, Murray, Wilson, Henderson, 
Nixon, &c. Eousseau and Schulten are classed as American 
players. The identity of each player is very thinly disguised 
by asterisks.

become famous in Chess literature, as writers, analysts, or players. 
Among these, V. d. Lasa, V. Bilguer, Hanstein, and Dr. Bledow 
are eminent, owing to their connection with the German HaTvdbuch 
des Schcbchspids. Petroff — a fine player — was the re-discoverer of 
the Petroff counter-attack, at one time regarded as a formidable 
rival to the Giuoco Piano. Jaenisch, a little later, published in his 
AnalT/se des Ouvertures an elaborate analysis of Petroff's variation. 
The French School, besides Labourdonnais, St. Amant, Descha- 
pelles, and Mouret, is represented by Boncourt, Devinck, L^crivain, 
Chamouillet, De la Eoche, and Bonfil. Calvi also comes in this 
category, while for central Europe we have Kieseritzky, a brilliant 
light in the openings, Zytogorsky, strong in analysis, Szen, a 
" deadly " end-game player, and Lowenthal, whose star had only 
just risen. The examples of play presented by Walker have 
obviously been chosen for their attractiveness from an English 
point of view. It is noteworthy that the German games are 
neither conspicuous for ponderous profundity, nor for severely 
scientific accuracy. Nevertheless Walker has remarked that 
nothing could be finer than some of the games incorporated 
played by V. d. Lasa, and it is probable that our readers will agree 
with him. He has also a good word for Dr. Bledow, whose play, 
in our young and Gambit days, was highly appreciated. 

The games in Chess Studies are all given without notes. Walker 
might well shrink from annotating, in the most cursory manner, 
such a vast collection. He occasionally adds the sign " ! " to a 
move, meaning thereby that to him it was astonishing and 
inexplicable. In other cases he subjoins, in italics, a move which 
he thought better than that which was really played. The student 
who needs a guide should first make himself acquainted with the 
style and standing of the players, after which he will have no 
difficulty in making his selections. Whatever his taste may be 
he ought to play over the games in Chapter I. If he admires 
daring adventures he will find plenty of them in the play of 
MacDonnell. Game No. 50 has never been surpassed in this respect. 
Genius and skill are combined in the practice of Labourdonnais. 
Both players seem to have looked with indifference on the loss of 
a pawn in the early part of a game. They had always before them 
the incalculable possibilities of the mid-game, and the chances of 
critical play in the endings. They played these as if thev loved 



xii INTRODUCTION. 

formed the subject of a *'poeme h6ro'i^<5omique " by M. M6ry, 
entitled Une revaTiche de Waterloo. According to the poet : 

*' Labourdonnais vainqueur, sort! de cette gaerre 
Fut sacre roi de France et roi de I'Angleterre 
Par le droit des eohecs." 

The attributes of later players, as shown in Chapter X., were 
less lofty but equally serviceable for the requirements of the 
time. Cochrane was an embodiment of boldness and cleverness ; 
Staunton combined skill with steadiness, studied development, 
held on to his pawns, and deprecated unnecessary risks and 
sacrifices. The student has only to choose for himself what 
style of play he most admires, or wishes to cultivate, and Chess 
Studies will furnish him with an ample supply of examples. 
The plodding player, the cautious player, the grasping player, 
the careless player — each man plays his part. None of them 
can easily conceal his characteristics. He is betrayed, in default 
of other evidence, by the kind of position into which his play 
leads him. Thus the most effective situations in Chapter I. are 
altogether different from those in Chapter IV. The chances for 
brilliant play that occurred to MacDonnell never occurred to 
Philidor, and this remark may be extended to players of inter- 
mediate styles. 

To a student of the Openings this work is of great value. At 
the time the games in Chapter X. were played, the main variations 
of the Giuoco Piano were well understood from the practice of the 
Italian masters. Since then, time has done little to advance its 
qualifications as a mode of development eligible for both players. 
(See Games Nos. 4, 551, 559.) Other openings have taken its. 
place in popular favour, and the Chess Studies show the crude 
shape in which these first presented themselves on the chess-board, 
and how rapidly the practice of strong players led to the adoption 
of finer forms of attack and defence. 

A chance discovery by Capt. Evans demonstrated that the 
Queen's Bishop had a latent power, of which the Evans Gambit 
and the Close Game are modem instances. The range of this work 
is also sufficient to enable us to trace the growth and development 
of the Scotch Gambit, named by Walker the ** Queen's Pawn Two 
Opening." Games Nos. 2, 3 show that Labourdonnais had the idea

INTRODUCTION. xiii 

probably intending to play sKt — KKtS, as in Game No. 91. 
MacDonnell prevented him by the reply 4 . . , Q — B3, and no 
further trial of this variation took place between them. Section 
II., in the tenth chapter, opens with other elementary forms, 
and leads us through various lines of play until MacDonnelFs move 
4 . . , Q — KB3 was superseded by 4 . . , B — B4 ; 5P — B3, Kt — 
KB3, Jaenisch's move, which transposed the opening into the 
Giuoco Rano. Here Walker left it. (See Game No. 660.) He 
objected to 4Kt takes P, the modem variation, as inferior to 4B — 
QB4, on account of Mr. PuUing's reply 4 . . . Q — KR5 (Game No. 
652) ; while 4 . . , B — QKt5 ch. was yet in question when Staunton 
published his Handbook in 1847. (See Games Nos. 667 to 701.) 

It was about 1834 that the popular and beautiful opening 
known as the Evans Gambit was introduced. Its first appearance 
in Chess Stvdies is in Game No. 26. Other games supply a 
complete synopsis of the experimental lines of play, which resulted 
in the well-known Normal Position after the first eight moves on 
both sides. It is reached by Labourdonnais in Game No. 56. The 
play subsequent to this point is chiefly tentative. One move 
highly favoured for the first player was 9P — KR3, which has now 
dropped out of ordinary practice. In the second form of the 
Evans Gambit, springing from move 5 . . , B — QR4, and leading 
into the variation known as the Compromised Defence, the furthest 
point reached in Chess Studies is found in Game No. 720, where 
Walker plays 6P — Q4. The move 6 Castles, was the first selected. 
It held its ground until 1847, when Mr. Stanley in the American 
Magazine suggested 6P — Q4 as stronger play. 

With regard to other Openings treated in Chapter X., the King's 
Bishop's Opening, and the Queen's Gambit Accepted — as well as the 
Giuoco Piano — have been very little affected by the lapse of time. 
In both, Chess Studies ' will yet, serve as a guide for practical 
purposes. In the King's Gambit Accepted and Declined, the 
Studies have only a historical value. The "King's Pawn One" 
game with its variations has been divided into several distinct 
openings — the French and Sicilian defences, the Queen's Gambit 
Declined, the King's Bishop's Pawn game, or Bird's Opening, &c. 
The Miscellaneous Openings in Chapter X., Section VII., include 
those now known as Philidor's Defence, the Greco Counter-Gambit, 
the Petroff Counter- Attack, Two Knights Defence, Ruy Lopez' 



634228 A 



xiv INTRODUCTION. 

Knight's Game, &c. All these have been fully discussed, and 
differently treated, since Walker's time. The games are not 
the less valuable as illustrations of mid-game combinations and 
end-game brilliancies. 

For mid-game study we have also the correspondence games in 
Chapter VII., and numerous games at odds. In the former, the 
additional time at the players' disposal usually leads them into 
playing a complicated game, turning upon . finer points than occur 
in ordinary play. For this reason every move is intended to 
bear the closest examination. In games at the odds of Pawn and 
one, or two moves, betwe^sn good players, assuming that the odds- 
receiver plays carefully, there usually comes a time when his 
opponent is tolerably clear of the pressure of difficulties incidental 
to the opening. This is the golden opportunity, with the man of 
witty inventions, for the imagination that foresees, the foresight 
that plans, the subtleties that baffle and confuse. He tries to 
break up his adversary's game by combinations. The preparatory 
moves leading to this end are worthy of special study, and the result- 
ing positions are sometimes of great beauty. Even when there are 
oversights it is not by any means uninteresting to note how they 
come to pass, inasmuch as it is sometimes necessary in practice to 
play for them, or at least to afford the other player chances. All 
this involves nice calculation. Blunders frequently arise through 
inconsiderate cupidity, also from weakness or obliquity of vision ; 
or the player may be paying more attention to his own game than 
to that of his opponent. There are, however, illustrations to the 
contrary: he may be either a pessimist or an optimist. The 
strength that sumves to the end-game is calmly contemplative, 
and declines to be moved by hopes or fears. Games Nos. 543, 
545, 107, 140, 146, 148, 297, will serve as illustrations. 

In those cases where the odds-giver has the first move in 
exchange for a piece, the whole field is open to him, but he has 
more force to overcome. His knowledge of the most attacking 
openings and gambits is then his strongest weapon. Chapters II., 
III., VIII., and IX. record many sparkling brevities. The games 
by Philidor, in Chapter IV., are, however, somewhat remarkable, 
because he does not take advantage of this resource, nor avoid ex- 
changes, which are usually thought to help the odds-receiver's game. 



INTRODUCTION. xv 

His play in the thirty-three games, Nos. 246-278, will give the 
modem student a more favourable idea of his great skill than 
those in which he played at shorter odds. Among his opponents, 
Walker names Count Briihl and Dr. Bowdler as the first amateurs 
in England: Dr. Bowdler specially commends himself by the 
charming game No. 291; Legalle (Game No. 306) was Philidor's 
instructor, and Verdoni his successor. Both of them gave odds to 
every English player. 

Among the multitudinous ways of winning a game of chess, 
Walker has given us a thousand instances. Every game has its 
ending, but this is hardly ever reached in precisely the same 
manner. We have both the dagger and the bowl — sudden death 
and prolonged agony. Some players prefer to win with their 
pieces, by repeated checks, sacrifices, and surprises, every piece 
taking its part in hurrying and worrying the adversary. Others 
rely on their pawn-play, and look with equanimity on all exchanges 
of pieces, which, without damaging their position, bring them 
nearer the end game. Philidor has always been considered the 
greatest authority on pawn-play extant. When playing on even 
terms he evaded the shock of contending pieces by moving his pawns 
to the front. In Game No. 220, the way in which he wins with his 
central pawns is very striking. His games are not so lively as 
those of the modem school, but they are capital studies notwith- 
standing. In Walker's day Philidor's method of handling his 
men had ceased to be popular. The forward attacking style of 
play which culminated in Morphy, as its finest exponent, was then 
in process of development. It certainly led to finer positions and 
more brilliant terminations. There has since been a partial reac- 
tion in favour of Philidor. Some eminent players so far follow 
him as to play for the end from the beginning. Their chief 
thought for every move is how it wiU affect the position in the 
end game. This was Philidor's principle, although the manner 
in which it is carried out in modem practice naturally varies from 
that favoured by him more than a century ago. 

Walker has left many games recorded as won, to be worked out 
in detail by his readers. On the other hand, he has continued 
many games far beyond the point where the win is obvious. In 
the former case a modem student will not have much diflSculty ; 



xvi INTRODUCTION. 

in the latter case he can always cease playing when satisfied. 
Most students, however, like to see a game conducted to the 
final mate. To them Chess Stvdies will prove a mine of pleasant 
surprises. 

The abbreviations used by Walker in this work are thus 
explained : w.-win, l.-lose, dr.-drawn, m.-mate, in.-interposes5 
att.-attacks, supp. -supports, en p. -en passant, q.-queens (applied to 
a pawn reaching its eighth square), perp. -perpetual, p. and m.-pawn 
and mov^, p. and 2 m.-pawn and two moves, m. in 3. -mate in three 
moves, doub.-doubled, &c. Whether a check be a double check 
or a check by discovery the same sign is applicable as to a single 
check. The notation is not the same as that ngw in use, but is 
easily understood, if the King's Knight and King's Eook are
